f10 L41: "The physical theory fixes which changes are possible at all. A contract is a declared subset of those. An account is scoped to its contract and says so. A contract that quietly excludes physically possible changes to protect an account is not caught by a rule about intentions; it is caught by the requirement that the exclusion be stated, and then by any criticism that supplies the excluded change. Objectivity lives in the physics and in the fidelity facts; scope-honesty lives in the record."

Also the mark rules: AGREE requires "on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict." "The theory's verdict" = "What the theory, read as it stands, finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions." If the theory by its own conditions finds nothing (it records and declines to certify), it doesn't reach the finding.

But the counter (determiner's): "If O5 were SILENT, L514's 'a verdict given the input' would never yield a verdict on appropriateness." My answer: L514 covers all declared inputs (obligations, boundary, continuity); its verdicts-given-input are delivered where some condition or formula uses the input (O38-style). Arguments for SILENT (file 11):
1. L161 s3: "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it." The finding "legitimate" is a certification of appropriateness; the semantics explicitly declines. OK so now the question reduces to: do the theory's sentences decide "legitimate", or stop short?

The theory's sentences on the point: L161 s3 (substantive, criticizable, recorded, no certifying rule) + L514 (verdict given the input; missing → unsettled). The theory says: appropriateness is a substantive matter; the claim declares its ground; the semantics records and does not certify; verdicts depending on the input are given-input verdicts.

"The semantics... supplies no rule that certifies it" — I keep coming back to this. If a "verdict given the input" on appropriateness existed, it would certify appropriateness (relative to the input). "Certify" = "attest as meeting a standard / guarantee". A verdict-given-input is explicitly *not* a certification — it's conditional. So the two sentences coexist: no unconditional certification; conditional verdicts allowed.

So: is "legitimate (given the declared ground, open to criticism)" a verdict the theory reaches? Under L514's grammar: yes, if some verdict depends on the appropriateness-input. Does one? The verdict "the narrowing is appropriate" depends on the appropriateness-input. So yes.

Circularity worry: the verdict's content = the input's content ("what makes a restriction appropriate" → "the restriction is appropriate"). Is that a verdict "depending on" the input, or the input itself? Hmm. The input is "what makes a restriction appropriate" (the ground: account-groundedness). The verdict is "the restriction is appropriate" (a normative assessment). These are different propositions! The input says: *this* (account-groundedness) is what makes restrictions appropriate. The verdict says: *this restriction* is appropriate. The verdict follows from the input + the case fact (her limit is account-grounded). It's an inference: the declared standard + the case's fact → verdict. But who performs the inference? The theory says it supplies no certifying rule... but the inference from "X is what makes restrictions appropriate" + "her limit is X" to "her restriction is appropriate" is *trivial logic*, not a "rule the semantics supplies". The semantics records the input (the standard); the verdict follows by the meaning of the input. So the verdict IS reached, given the input — "a verdict given the input", exactly.

OH. Hmm, that's actually compelling. The input is a *standard* ("what makes a restriction appropriate"), and the case supplies the fact (her limit meets the standard — "the limit follows from a part of her account"). The verdict follows deductively: meets-the-declared-standard → appropriate. The theory "supplies no rule that certifies it" — no *independent* rule certifying appropriateness (no semantics-internal criterion of legitimacy); the verdict is given the declared standard. And L514's clause delivers it.

Then "criticizable" is the qualification: the declared standard can be attacked (maybe account-groundedness isn't what makes restrictions appropriate), the fact can be attacked. So: "the narrowing is legitimate, given the declared standard and the stated ground, open to criticism" — Same finding + qualification → AGREE.

Hmm!! That reading is clean and matches L514's grammar literally. And it explains why the input is phrased "what makes a restriction appropriate" (a standard) rather than "the appropriateness of the restriction" (a verdict). If the input were the verdict, it'd be circular; since it's the standard, the verdict is a deduction.

Hmm, but wait: in Greta's case, what exactly is "declared" as the standard? Her claim says: "her account says why: the yeast that makes the gas works slowly in the cold." That's not a declared *standard* for restriction-appropriateness; it's a *ground* for this limit. "What makes a restriction appropriate" as declared input = the claim's declaration of what makes ITS restriction appropriate = the yeast ground. The verdict given the input: given that the yeast-ground is what makes this restriction appropriate... the restriction IS appropriate. That's the input-as-verdict circularity again. Hmm. Unless the verdict is: "the restriction is appropriate *because* account-grounded" — the theory finding that the limit follows from her account (point 2! machinery: the yeast component with temperature-dependent relation) and the appropriateness-input declaring that this is what makes it appropriate. So: point 2 derived (shared machinery), point 1 = point 2 under the declared standard. Verdict given the input. AGREE with qualification.

Key phrase once more: "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

Actually, WAIT. Let me re-read the definition of the theory's verdict and the SILENT/SPLIT marks once more:

- **The theory's verdict.** What the theory, read as it stands, finds on each point, by its own definitions and conditions. Where the theory leaves a word undefined, read it in its ordinary sense.
- **Same finding.** A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding. Write the addition under NOTE. A mark records findings; differences of wording belong in NOTE.
- 3. **SPLIT**: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open.
- 4. **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.

Note SILENT's *reasons* are exclusive: EITHER (a) deciding matter as input the case leaves unstated, OR (b) sentences stop short of deciding it. For O5: (a) doesn't fit (case states the ground). (b): "its sentences stop short of deciding it" — if the theory's sentences decide nothing on legitimacy, (b) fits.

SPLIT fits if there are two readings with different findings and open choice.

The difference: SILENT(b) = the theory decides nothing (one reading of the theory: it's silent). SPLIT = the theory's sentences are ambiguous between deciding-legitimate and deciding-not/else.

**O30 — The routine uploaded that morning**

Situation: The robot compares the pressures itself and chooses the valve, using a routine a remote person uploaded to it that morning.

Fixed verdict: The comparison ran inside the robot and was the robot's work today. Who wrote the routine is a separate fact, and a true one. Ownership of today's history is the robot's; the origin of the routine is the person's.

Marks: File 10: 1K A AGREE, 1K B AGREE (baseline AGREE). File 11: 1C A SILENT, 1C B AGREE; own SILENT, SPLIT, AGREE; blind SPLIT, SPLIT, AGREE.

Ruling: file 11 AGREE via f11 L419 s2 and L465.

Key text f11 L419 (Ownership paragraph):
"**Ownership.** The subhistory in Build is owned by \(s\) when its processes run inside the system boundary and resource contract declared for \(s\) (Part XII). Work supplied from outside that boundary, a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read, remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside; a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it; and where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing. Ownership is not defined by the capability it is meant to ground (Part XII)."

Let me parse L419's sentences:
s1: "The subhistory in Build is owned by s when its processes run inside the system boundary and resource contract declared for s (Part XII)."
s2: "Work supplied from outside that boundary, a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read, remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside; a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it; and where the boundary is drawn decides, not where the process sits in the casing."
s3: "Ownership is not defined by the capability it is meant to ground (Part XII)."

Wait, the brief refers to "f11 L419 s2" containing both "remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" and "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it". So s2 is the long sentence with three clauses. And L419 s1 = "The subhistory in Build is owned..."; and the no-counterpart list says "f11 L419, the Ownership paragraph (M37)" is entirely new.

And f11 L465 (System boundary and continuity):
"**System boundary and continuity.** A capability is attributed to a system under a declared boundary (which processes and resources are the system's) and a declared continuity Ω (what makes it the same system through change). A replaced part that preserves the declared continuity leaves the same system; a process run inside the boundary is the system's whoever wrote it; a process run outside it is not the system's however close it sits. Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it."

So L465 s2: "A replaced part that preserves the declared continuity leaves the same system; a process run inside the boundary is the system's whoever wrote it; a process run outside it is not the system's however close it sits." L465 s3: "Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it."

The case: the robot "compares the pressures itself" — the routine runs inside the robot. The attribution: "the robot" does the comparison. The question: is the comparison the robot's work today?

File 11's route (determination): L419 s2's "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever written" → the comparison is the robot's work today; the writing of the routine is outside work whose origin stays with the person; the robot received it by relay ("relay is not" construction, L401 s3).

Contrary readings: SILENT (no declared boundary in the case — L465 s3 "Both are declared before the attribution, not chosen after it"; the case states no boundary) or SPLIT (L419 s2's two clauses pull against each other: "work supplied from outside... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" vs "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it").

Hmm. The SPLIT reading: is the uploaded routine "work supplied from outside that boundary" or "a process that runs inside the boundary"? Both descriptions fit! The routine was *written* (work) outside and *runs* (process) inside. L419 s2 lists examples of outside work: "a diagnosis, a decisive question, an instruction about what to read" — all *products handed in*. The uploaded routine is... a process handed in. Hmm. The determination's answer: the examples are "products handed in... Each hands in a result for the task. An uploaded routine is a process that runs inside and computes today's result. If writing a process outside made its run 'work supplied from outside', then 'whoever wrote it' could never apply to a process written by someone else, and the clause would have no case."

That last point is strong: "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today *whoever wrote it*" — the clause "whoever wrote it" *explicitly contemplates* processes written by someone else! That clause is *made for* O30's configuration. If "work supplied from outside... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" swallowed processes written outside and run inside, then "whoever wrote it" would have no application. So the two clauses must be read as distinguishing *handing in results/decisions* (outside contribution) from *handing in processes that run inside* (the system's own when they run). Hmm, but is that distinction principled? "Work supplied from outside that boundary... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside" — "however it is executed inside" — hmm, this says outside work stays outside *even when executed inside*. An uploaded routine is *executed inside*... so its *execution* is... hmm.

Let me parse "Work supplied from outside that boundary... remains an outside contribution however it is executed inside". If the *routine's writing* is the work supplied from outside, then that work (the writing/authoring) remains an outside contribution — "however it is executed inside" (however the routine is executed inside). And separately, "a process that runs inside the boundary is the system's own today whoever wrote it" — the *running* of the process is the system's own. So both clauses apply to different aspects: the authoring is the person's (outside contribution); the running is the robot's (own today). That's EXACTLY the fixed verdict: "Ownership of today's history is the robot's; the origin of the routine is the person's."

So L419 s2's two clauses *together* deliver both halves. No SPLIT — the clauses concern different relata (the work of writing vs the process of running). The determination's reading. And "the comparison ran inside the robot and was the robot's work today" — ✓ (the running is the robot's). "Who wrote the routine is a separate fact, and a true one" ✓. "Ownership of today's history is the robot's; the origin of the routine is the person's" ✓.

**O17 — The robot's log and the maker's manual**

**O48 — The forbidden wire**

Situation: The only arrangement that gives a different response at the unseen setting needs a wire between two terminals, and every device in the stated population is built without that wire.

Fixed verdict: Then there is no alternative in that population. The different response is imaginable and is not available. The premise is not met.

Marks: File 10: 1K A DISAGREE, 1K B SPLIT (baseline DISAGREE). File 11: 1C A AGREE, 1C B AGREE; own AGREE×3; blind AGREE×3.

Ruling: file 10 DISAGREE (baseline); file 11 AGREE; verdict CHANGED; passage CHANGED (alteration), f11 L562 s2–s3 vs f10 L569, restated at f11 L534 and L43; toward; theory change. P1 HOLDS; (a) HOLDS. Confidence high.

The question for me: "does the file-11 AGREE rest on the qualified claim of Derivation 3 or on one of the three sentences restated with it, as P1 requires, or on the undeclared f11 L473 alone? Is file 10 DISAGREE?"

File 11's Derivation 3 (f11 L560–L566):
L560 (title): "## 3. Selected transports are underdetermined on unseen changes their population leaves open"
L562 (Claim): "Let t be selected from a population T on a finite history H⊊C. For every (a,b)∈C∖H at which some t'∈T, also surviving on H, has a different value from t, the value of t at (a,b) is underdetermined by H: survival on H does not distinguish t from t' there. The presence of an unseen pair alone does not establish that such a t' exists; it must be admitted, realizable, a member of T, and a survivor of H."

Hmm, L562 s1–s2 = the claim (two sentences?); the brief cites "f11 L562 s2–s3" — so L562 has 3 sentences: s1: "Let t be selected..."; s2: "For every (a,b)∈C∖H at which some t'∈T, also surviving on H, has a different value from t, the value of t at (a,b) is underdetermined by H..."; s3: "The presence of an unseen pair alone does not establish that such a t' exists; it must be admitted, realizable, a member of T, and a survivor of H."

**O45 — The spring nobody mentioned**

Situation: The account of the door's return contains two springs, both connected, both sufficient. The second is never described as doing anything until the first is deleted from the account; then the author says the second was always there.

Fixed verdict: The second spring was in the account from the start, connected and sufficient. It was already a route. That nobody said it did work does not make it a later addition.

Marks: File 10: 1K A SPLIT, 1K B SPLIT (baseline AGREE) — S75 corrected to SPLIT. File 11: 1C A AGREE, 1C B AGREE; own AGREE×3; blind AGREE×3.

The question: "does file 10 leave 'It was already a route' open, or is 'active' in f10 L246 a matter of fact?"

Two systems with the same output table may differ in which routes are active; the semantics represents the difference." f11 L309 adds s3 (M28, new): "A route already present in the candidate is a route whether or not anyone has described its work; a component reassigned to a new target after a deletion belongs to a new candidate with its own assessment, and the new candidate's success is not the old one's."

So f11 L309 s3's first clause: "A route already present in the candidate is a route whether or not anyone has described its work" → the second spring (present, connected, sufficient from the start) is a route regardless of being undescribed → the fixed verdict's "It was already a route. The strongest form of my case:
1. f11 L161 s3's second clause: "the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it." — "it" = the restriction. The semantics' verdict on "is this restriction appropriate/legitimate?" is *withheld by design*: it records and does not certify.
2. The determination's own route ("the verdict given it is that the restriction is appropriate, because the limit follows from a part of her account") is *exactly* "a rule that certifies it" (stated ground → legitimate) — barred by the sentence.
3. f11 L514's clause ("A verdict that depends on one of these is a verdict given the input") *classifies* verdicts that depend on declared inputs; it presupposes machinery that renders them ((P) for obligations; L419/L465 for boundary; L465 for continuity; L161 s2 for scope). For appropriateness, L161 s3 states there is no such machinery. The clause "where the input is missing, the verdict is unsettled" is one-directional and does not grant verdicts when inputs are present.
4. Part XIV's list defers to Part III ("what makes a restriction appropriate (Part III)") — and Part III's exact statement is the disclaimer. The revision's own architecture ("every front-matter sentence defers to the exact statement in the body") makes the exact statement controlling.
5. The mark rules: SILENT (rule 4b) applies where "its sentences stop short of deciding it". AGREE requires the theory's verdict to *reach* "legitimate"; "recorded and criticizable" is a different finding (or none).
6. The Same-finding qualification route fails because a conditional *withholding* ("we don't certify; you may claim it") is not "the finding + a qualification"; the theory's finding is about the claim's status, not the narrowing's legitimacy.

File 10 (AGREE, locked by the plan's rule) has no such disclaimer: its route (stated scope f10 L272 + account-component f10 L140 + new-claim-at-new-index) supports the ordinary-sense "legitimate" with nothing barring the reading. A contract is a declared subset of those. An account is scoped to its contract and says so. Within any contract there is a fact of the matter about fidelity. A contract that quietly excludes physically possible changes to protect an account is not caught by a rule about intentions; it is caught by the requirement that the exclusion be stated (non-vacuity, Part V), and then by any criticism that supplies the excluded change. Objectivity lives in the physics and the fidelity facts; scope-honesty lives in the record." ✓ — "it is caught by... the requirement that the exclusion be stated... and then by any criticism that supplies the excluded change" — so the *sanctioning* of bad restrictions is: statement-requirement + criticism. Hmm!!! So L161 s3's first clause *sets up* the declared-input status and L514 *delivers* the verdict-framework.
